Enthroned above all creation towers the exalted, glorified Christ. Descending into the darkest recesses of human agony and sin reaches the warm, caring Jesus. These two are the same person. Lukeâs testimony introduces us to this man become GodâGod the Son. He comes into our world already bearing a divine nature, already carrying divine qualities. His birth is a miracle; he is âChrist the Lordâ (Luke 2:11).

The most distinguishing element of this line-by-line, word-by-word commentary is its use of Latter-Â­day Saint scripturesâthe Book of Mormon, the Doctrine and CoveÂ­nants, and the Pearl of Great Priceâto illuminate Lukeâs Gospel. For example, important LDS doctrines arise from Jesusâ activity in the spirit world immediately after his death.

More than all other Gospel accounts, Luke captures the compassion and love of the Savior. Such sweet concern manifests itself particularly for the downtrodden and those forced to the margins of society. Within his text, Luke discloses the deep, divine love that runs through his narrative of the Christ.

S. Kent Brown combines a lifetime of dedicated study of the ancient world with his reverence for the Bible and insights from restoration scripture to create a readable, relevant, and thought-provoking commentary on the Gospel according to Luke. Beautifully written with a unique sensitivity toward Jesusâ focus on family relationships, the sanctity of the home, and the dangers of materialism, this book invites&1e:T37745,

Introduction

Above all, the Atonement begins. Almost as soon as the Savior walks into Gethsemane, he is rocked by such deep anguish that he begs his Father, âremove this cup from meâ (22:42). In response, his Father sends âan angel unto him from heaven,â not to lead him away from his suffering but to strengthen him (22:43). Within moments, the suffering is so intense that Jesusâ âsweat [becomes] as it were great drops of blood falling down to the groundâ (22:44). Uncontrollably, he bleeds from âevery poreâ of his body (Mosiah 3:7; D&C 19:18). But his path does not end there.

Ever looking for opportunity, even in his suffering, the Savior later makes the council chamber filled with enemies into the place of his last witness of who he is (see the Analysis on 22:63â71). Some of the titles come from the lips of Sanhedrin members, others from Jesusââthe Christ,â âthe Son of man,â âthe Son of God,â the ineffable âI amâ (22:67, 69â70). All these exalted terms gather with such force that each of the council members has to make an inward decision about this man, whether to accept or reject him.1 In Lukeâs reporting, only one is touched, Joseph of Arimathea, an acquaintance of Jesus (see 23:50â51; the Note on 23:50).

Deep currents run through this chapter, not surprising in light of the turbulent stream of events flowing through Jesusâ last hours. One has to do with the Apostles. On the negative side, âIf anything can go wrong, it will.â First, Judas reaches out to the authorities and negotiates a deal to deliver Jesus to them (see 22:3â6). Second, during the Passover supper that Jesus hosts, a rancorous debate breaks out among the Apostles about who is most important, a debate that he has to calm (see 22:24â27). Either of these incidents might tend to fracture the fellowship of the Twelve, unhinging the sacred circle. But the first incident is more galling because Judasâs act stands on a deeper malicious footing than the discord among the Twelve; his betrayal is far more odious and serious because he is about to turn against friends with whom he still shares table fellowship.2 Concerning the scene of strife, it seems to intrude textually into Lukeâs account, chiefly because a similar scene appears much earlier in other Gospel reports (see Matt. 20:20â28; Mark 10:35â45). But no strong reason emerges to see the incident as unhistorical. Instead, it becomes the launch pad for Jesusâ last earthly instructions to his beloved Apostles about divine service and about their celestial futures (see 22:25â30). Onto the positive end also spill the happy preparations for the meal (see 22:7â13) and the meal itself, which Jesus turns into a remembrance of his body and blood (see 22:14â20). In this connection, Lukeâs record of the institution of the sacrament agrees remarkably with the information that comes to the Apostle Paul and with the eyewitness account of the same institutional ceremony in Third Nephi (see 1 Cor. 11:23â25; 3 Ne. 18:1â13; 20:3â9).

A second surging current carries Satan back into the story. He appears first in this Gospel to entice Jesus with temptations in the wilderness. There he is called âthe devil,â with a hint that he will come back (4:1â13). Now he returns. While the devil has been absent from the narrative, Jesus receives the Seventy disciples back from their missions. Because of their successes, he declares to them that he beholds Satan âas lightning fall from heaven,â a sure defeat for the devil (10:18). Then, in chapter 22, Satan astonishingly finds a willing host among the Twelve and influences Judas both to turn against his master and to betray him into the hands of conspiring authorities (see 22:3â4). But Jesus is not caught unawares because earlier, without mentioning any name, he prophesies about Judasâs surprising and puzzling action (see 9:44; 18:31â33). Still, in his words to Peter, the Savior discloses information that he accesses by divine means only, revealing that Satan asks for and receives permission to try not only Peter but also âthe children of the kingdomâ (JST 22:31; see the Note on 22:31).

In a third rushing tide, the themes of death, suffering (see 22:15), and blood push into the narrative, now in connection with the slaughter of lambs in preparation for the Passover. This recalls motifs that we first see in chapter 2 when Mary and Joseph take Jesus to the templeâimplicit in this earlier scene are the death and blood of sacrificial animals on the temple grounds (see 2:22â24; 22:7; the Analysis on 2:8â20). In the earlier case, these inflections in the narrative only hint at Jesusâ distant Atonement. Now, in chapter 22, they point vividly to the Atonement which is near at hand, particularly his anguished bleeding in Gethsemane (see 22:44). In this chapter, these themes form a steady stream because they tie directly to the Passover, the commemoration of the night that the firstborn Hebrew slaves live while firstborn Egyptians die.

A fourth currentâthe sustenance of life by eatingâis closely tied by contrasting links to the motif of death. How so? Much of the chapter treats the supper that Jesus and his Apostles share, with references throughout to eating and drinking that include the table where they share the meal as well as the bitter âcupâ of Gethsemane (see 22:21, 42). But this meal is unusual. Indeed, it is made sacred and enduring by Jesusâ presence, by his words of blessing, and by actions of giving (see 22:17, 19â20). More than this, the meal celebrates not only the Passover (see the Analysis on 22:7â13) that points backward in time to the Lordâs sustaining of the Hebrewsâ lives in the desert by the gifts of manna and water, but forward to the messianic banquet when Jesus will sit as host with those in his kingdom who then enjoy eternal life (see 22:16, 18, 30; Ex. 15:22â25; 16:1â31; 17:1â6; Num. 20:1â11). Both events have to do with life. At the center of this celebration, Jesus transforms the Passover, one of the most memorable commemorations among his people, and institutes the sacrament, a sacred sharing of bread and wine in memory of his body and blood as the physical promises of a blessed life in the future (see 22:19â20).

Fifth, in this chapter Jesus announces to his disciples that there will soon be an almost cataclysmic tidal change in the propagation of the gospel, ranking with Jesusâ announcement of his ministry in his hometown of Nazareth (see 4:16â21) and his turning away from Galilee toward Jerusalem (see 9:51). In the midst of the Last Supper, before warning Peter and his fellow Apostles about Satanâs designs on them and before prophesying about Peterâs denials (see 22:31â34), he announces that their ministries will soon change. Rather than enjoying their fellowship with him in relative calm, they will soon experience persecution and affliction, days wherein they will be carried into âsynagogues, and into prisons, being brought before kings and rulersâ (21:12). For Jesusâ ministry, âthe things concerning [him] have an endâ; and that alteration will impact the Apostles deeply (22:35â38). Times are about to change.

Finally, although the Joseph Smith Translation touches Lukeâs account minimally, it adds abundant detail to the reports of Matthew and Mark, bringing both clarity and a divine perspective to the record (see the Notes on 22:19â20).

Translation Notes and Comments

22:1 feast of unleavened bread: This agriculture feast, paired with the Passover, is thought to gain its prominence after the Israelites settle on land that they can farm (see Lev. 23:4â8; Num. 28:17â25).3

called the Passover: Scripturally, this festival originates with the Exodus of the Hebrew slaves from Egypt (see Ex. 12:1â20). Long before Jesusâ day, the Passover is fused with the Feast of Unleavened Bread into a week-long commemoration.4

22:2 chief priests and scribes: Luke repeats the identity of those who oppose Jesus at the beginning of the week (see 19:47; 20:1, 19). They do not give up. The chief priests, or high priests (see 3:2), include the presiding high priest Caiaphas (AD 18â36) and his cohorts (see Matt. 26:3; John 11:49; 18:13; Acts 4:6).5 These people effectively run the city, from the Jewish side, after the death of Herod and the banishment of his son Archelaus, holding control over the temple and the Sanhedrin.6 Long before, Jesus prophesies that he will âbe rejected of the . . . chief priests and scribes, and be slainâ (9:22). As a group, the scribes first appear as associates of the Pharisees (see the Notes on 5:21 and 5:30). The Pharisees, last identified among the voices heard from the crowd that accompanies Jesus from Jericho (see 19:39), now disappear from the narrative.

kill him: To eliminate Jesus is the main objective of these people from the moment that he strides into Jerusalem (see 19:47; 20:19â20; also 2 Ne. 10:3). Lukeâs statement places Judasâs next action into a context that welcomes his initiative (see 22:3â5).

22:3 Then entered Satan into Judas: The words plainly denote that Satan himself enters the physical being of Judas (see John 13:2, 27; on Satanâs ability to influence, see Alma 5:41; 34:35; 40:13; D&C 10:10, 15, 20â21; 29:40). Throughout his ministry, Jesus is forced as it were to deal again and again with evil spirits. On this occasion, when Satan knows that he is in a position to hurt the Savior, both by compromising one of his trusted associates and by sending Jesus to the cross, he does not send one of his lieutenants to tend to business. He himself comes to Jerusalem (see the Notes on 22:31, 53). In all, we sense that Judas is doing more than simply trying to steer Jesus into a confrontation with authorities, but his motives remain out of sight.7 Judas must feel a high degree of loyalty and love for the Savior. And for him to take the step of betrayal needs enticement from Satan. Even so, the Lord does not remove the guilt in the least (see John 17:12; 3 Ne. 27:32; 29:7). The possibility that Judas acts at Jesusâ direction so that authorities will surely arrest him, as the story is portrayed in the apocryphal Gospel of Judas, is without historical basis.8

Judas surnamed Iscariot: This man, though mentioned often in the Gospels and elsewhere (see 6:16; 22:47, 48; Matt. 10:4; Mark 3:19; John 6:71; Acts 1:16, 25; etc.), remains enigmatic. Perhaps oddly, this passage introduces Judas for a second time, because Lukeâs narrative introduces him initially at the time of his call, perhaps pointing to a different source (see 6:16). The meaning of Judasâs surname, Iscariot, receives attention mainly because its origin is uncertain. It may mean something like âman from Keriothâ which may be a town south of Jerusalem, but this is unsure (see the Note on 6:16; Josh. 15:25).9

being of the number of the twelve: In writing this line, Luke underscores in deep colors the base wickedness of Judasâs betrayalâhe turns both against his Lord and Master as well as against his fellow members of the Twelve, all of whom belong to Jesus,10 shattering their fellowship borne and nourished over long months (see 22:47).

22:4 went his way: The basic meaning of the Greek verb aperchomai is âto go away, to depart,â indicating an intentional withdrawal (see 1:23, 38; 2:15; 5:25; 7:24; etc.).11 In our mindâs eye, we can see Judas taking the initiative at first opportunity and purposely slinking away from the company of Jesus and the Twelve to meet the authorities, an act that propels him outside his former fellowship.

communed . . . how he might betray him unto them: Judasâs discussion with the authorities, all from the temple, is thorough, as the Greek verb sullaleÅ indicates (see 9:30; Acts 25:12),12 and must include settling the needed arrangements which will allow him to deliver the Savior to them out of the sight of Jesusâ numerous supporters (see 22:6). The Greek verb paradidÅmi, translated âto betray,â bears this sense in a number of other passages (see 9:44; 18:32; 22:6, 21â22, 48; 24:7).13

captains: These officers are Jewish and, working under the direction of a chief captain, bear responsibility for keeping order within the extensive temple grounds (see Acts 4:1; 5:24, 26; etc.; the Note on 22:52).14 That Judas confers with temple authorities illustrates how thoroughly the established representatives of Godâs house resist the Savior.15

22:5 were glad: The verb (Greek chairÅ) is stronger than this translation and means âto rejoiceâ at the opportunity that Judasâs act affords to them.16 In this portrait, Luke captures the perverse joy that the authorities experience at the opportunity to destroy Jesus. No wonder he withholds his forgiveness on the cross (see JST 23:35; the Note on 23:34).

covenanted to give him money: The amount, thirty pieces of silver, is preserved by Matthewâs narrative (see Matt. 26:15; 27:3, 9). The sense of the middle voice of the verb (Greek suntithÄmi) is âto agree,â17 which appears here in the simple past tense and specifies a one-time meeting to reach an agreement.

22:6 he promised: The active verb appears only here in the New Testament (Greek exomologeÅ) and carries the sense âto consent, to promiseâ in a negotiated agreement.18 The Joseph Smith Translation adds the pronoun âthem,â emphasizing Judasâs personal contact with the authorities (JST 22:6).

sought opportunity: In contrast to the mood of the one-time meeting and negotiation, the imperfect mood of the verb (Greek zÄteÅ) points to Judasâs continuous efforts to find a way to turn Jesus over to the authorities.19

in the absence of the multitude: In the minds of officials, this condition is the key feature in their agreement with Judas, that they can make an arrest away from the public gaze (see 19:47â48; 20:19).

Analysis

At last, the drama of the Saviorâs Atonement commences, breaching the dam of seeming inaction. The scene opens on one of the most venerated of festivals, the Passover. Later in the day, Jesus will recline with his most beloved associates, the Twelve, for a private supper together. Unseen by all, except Jesus and Judas, the final conspiratorial act that will lead to Jesusâ betrayal and death surges forward. This act will define Judas Iscariot forever as the one who sells out his Master to the Masterâs tireless enemies. Luke does not offer an overt judgment against Judas, but his narrative summary of Judasâs meeting with the authorities tells the tale of Judasâs perfidy (see 22:4â6). It is other sources that condemn Judas in the strongest terms, calling him a âson of perditionâ (see John 17:12; 3 Ne. 27:32; 29:7; also D&C 76:31â38, 43â48).

Importantly, Satan comes to Jerusalem to handle the final details of the effort to undercut the Saviorâs influence among citizens and visitors alike. He himself is missing in action since his encounter with the Savior in the wilderness at the end of the forty-day fast (see 4:1â13). But now he returns in time to assault Judas and influence him to turn to his will, demonstrating his enormous ability to affect those who leave themselves open to his power (see John 13:2, 27; Jacob 7:18; Alma 30:53; 3 Ne. 1:22; 2:2â3; D&C 10:10, 15, 20â21; 29:40; 52:14).

The negotiation between Judas and the authoritiesâwe have to call the meeting a negotiation because such parleys lie at the base of all deals in the ancient Near Eastâinvolves a number of important stipulations that the parties work out during their single meeting together (see the Note on 22:4). On Judasâs side, he makes the initial contact and tenders the offer to betray Jesus into their hands (see 22:4). Furthermore, in the encounter he pledges to carry out his end of the agreement with a firm promise, likely uttering an oath,20 and, doubtless at their insistence, he vows to turn Jesus over in a setting without crowds, something that worries them (see 22:6). On the authoritiesâ side, they agreeâprobably with an oathâto pay money to Judas when he delivers Jesus (see 22:5). This meeting turns the tide. And this meeting uncovers their perverse joy at the prospect of finally ridding themselves of Jesus (see the Note on 22:5).

Preparations for the Passover Meal(22:7â13)(Compare Matt. 26:17â19; Mark 14:12â16)

King James Translation

7 Then came the day of unleavened bread, when the passover must be killed. 8 And he sent Peter and John, saying, Go and prepare us the passover, that we may eat. 9 And they said unto him, Where wilt thou that we prepare? 10 And he said unto them, Behold, when ye are entered into the city, there shall a man meet you, bearing a pitcher of water; follow him into the house where he entereth in. 11 And ye shall say unto the goodman of the house, The Master saith unto thee, Where is the guestchamber, where I shall eat the passover with my disciples? 12 And he shall shew you a large upper room furnished: there make ready. 13 And they went, and found as he had said unto them: and they made ready the passover.

[/column1] [column2]

New Rendition

7 And the day of unleavened bread came, in which it is necessary to sacrifice the paschal lamb. 8 And he sent Peter and John saying, âGo, prepare the Passover for us in order that we might eat.â 9 And they said to him, âWhere do you wish that we prepare it?â 10 And he said to them, âBehold, after you enter the city, a man carrying a pitcher of water will meet you. Follow him into the house that he enters, 11 and say to the master of the house, âThe teacher says to you, âWhere is the dining room where I may eat the Passover with my disciples?ââ 12 And that man will show you a large, furnished upstairs room. There prepare it.â 13 After departing, they found it just as he had said to them. And they prepared the Passover.

Translation Notes and Comments

22:7 came the day: Even though readers will naturally understand this as a reference to the first day of the unleavened bread celebration (see Ex. 12:15) and to the Passover festival, this and similar expressions in other contextsâoften âthe days comeââdisclose a sudden change that requires a decision (see Isa. 39:6; the Notes on 6:23; 17:22; 19:42, 43). Jesusâ suffering and death will usher in the need for the decision whether to embrace him as the Messiah or not.

the day of unleavened bread . . . the passover: Luke, or his source, evidently conflates two separate events. The day of preparation when âthe passover must be killedâ is on the 14th day of the month of Nissan, whereas the first day of the Feast of Unleavened Bread falls on the 15th of Nissan, a Jewish calendrical nicety that a gentile author might miss (see Num. 28:16â17).21

the passover: The unspoken referent is to the lamb, or kid,22 that will grace each personâs table for the feast in accord with Mosaic law (see 22:15; Ex. 12:2, 21).

must be killed: The slaughter of lambs for the Passover always takes place in the afternoon, never in the morning.23 This expression may frame Lukeâs only allusion to Jesus as the paschal lamb (see the Note on 2:8). The verb translated âmustâ (Greek dei) often nods toward the divine necessity that guides Jesusâ ministry (see 2:49; 4:43; 13:16; the Note on 19:5), particularly his suffering (see 13:33; 22:37; 24:7, 26, 44, 46; the Notes on 9:22; 17:25).24 Coupled with this observation stands the meaning of the second verb (Greek thuÅ) that is often rendered âto killâ but regularly bears the primary sense âto sacrificeâ (see Acts 14:13, 18; 1 Cor. 5:7; 10:20).25

22:8 he sent Peter and John: We meet the verb earlier (Greek apostellÅ) as one that empowers an official representative (see 11:49; 22:35; the Notes on 4:18; 9:2, 52; 10:1, 3). Only Luke names these disciples. Matthew reports the involvement of several followers (see Matt. 26:17â19) and Mark mentions two unnamed disciples (see Mark 14:13). Incidentally, it is customary for a group to send one or two persons to the temple to take care of the formalities associated with the slaughter of the Passover lamb.26 Though missing from the reports, we must assume that some of Jesusâ women followers involve themselves in the preparations for the meal (see the Note on 22:13). For they follow him from Galilee and we see them taking a part in his burial (see 23:49, 55â56; 24:1, 10). They will not be willing to stand on the sidelines while the men alone take care of all the arrangements. Some are wealthy, influential women who regularly provide for the Savior and his followers (see 8:2â3). As a group, they are probably participants in his budding church, as Lukeâs notices plainly hint (see the Notes on 8:1â3; 23:56; the Analysis on 23:50â56). Not incidentally, Jesusâ conversation with Peter and John takes place outside the city, apparently as they walk toward it (see 22:10).

prepare us the passover: Preparations include gathering the foods for the meal, especially purchasing a lamb at the temple. If they bring their own to the temple for slaughter, they are allowed into the Court of Israel, the area beyond the Nicanor Gate, to observe the preparation of their lamb and to participate in singing the traditional Hallel songs of thanksgiving with the Levite choir (see the Note on 2:42). In addition, Jerusalem officials place ovens throughout the city so that pilgrims like Jesus and his followers can cook their lambs according to biblical law (see Ex. 12:5â10).27

that we may eat: This is only the second meal in the Gospel that Jesus himself initiates, besides the feeding of the five thousand (see 9:16â17), hinting at its special character. In all other scenes that Luke sketches, Jesus dines as a guest (see 7:36; 10:38â40; 11:37; 14:1; the Notes on 19:5, 6). To enjoy a meal with the Savior recalls all the blessings of abundance that Jehovah bestows on his people.28

22:9 they said . . . Where: Jesus gives just enough information to the two Apostles to stir them to action. But he withholds essential information so that, if they are willing to obey, they must ask for further direction. This observation holds true across the spectrum for anyone who is willing to obey the Savior. Importantly, the question assumes that the Apostles know the city well and can find their way to any locale,29 including the upscale part on the west side of town. It also hints that they have been guests in homes there and not mere onlookers. This observation helps to settle the question whether Jesus and these followers travel to Jerusalem and its environs on several prior occasions. Even though Luke explicitly records only one visit to the city during Jesusâ ministry, the final one (see the Notes on 10:30, 38; 13:1, 34; 19:47; 23:5, 28, 50; 24:13; the Analysis below),30 Johnâs Gospel records several visits (see John 2:13; 5:1; 7:10; 11:17â18; 12:12â13), This omission by Luke is to be joined with others (see the Notes on 5:6, 11; 9:33; 19:5; the Introduction II.B and III.B).

22:10 when ye are entered into the city: The assignment points Peter and John to one of the gates. Evidently, the gate is not one that leads directly into the temple area because the man with the pitcher is inside the city. Further, he is not within the temple grounds either carrying water away from or into the complex, unless he is a priest, and no hint appears that he is. Instead, he bears some relationship to the owner of the house such as a hireling or a servant (see 22:10â11).

a man . . . bearing a pitcher of water: The man will catch the attention of Peter and John because women normally carry such pitchers (see John 4:7, 28).31 The fact that this man is probably carrying water to a home indicates that the home does not have running water but relies on cisterns to store bathing and cleaning water, and on water storage tanks for culinary and drinking water, as archaeology shows.32

the house: The Last Supper is to take place in a room that forms part of the structure of a home (see the Notes below on 22:11, 12).

22:11 the goodman of the house: Evidently, this man is a disciple because he knows Jesus by the simple yet respectful title âthe Master.â He remains unnamed in the synoptic Gospels perhaps because of the need to keep him anonymous in the face of threats from authorities in the city.33 How many more disciples reside in Jerusalem we cannot know. But we note with interest that this unnamed disciple owns the home. We meet this term (Greek oikodespotÄs) often in Jesusâ sayings and it generally refers to a person of means (see 12:39; 13:25; 14:21).34

The Master: This title of respect (Greek didaskalos) appears elsewhere in Lukeâs account as a title for Jesus and others, but not on Jesusâ lips as a designation for himself (see 8:49; 9:38; 10:25; 18:18; etc.; the Note on 7:40). Here, for the only time in Lukeâs record, Jesus applies the title to himself. In its fundamental sense, the Greek title means âteacher.â35

the guestchamber: The Greek term (katalyma) is the same as that translated âinnâ in the story of Joseph and Mary seeking shelter in Bethlehem (2:7). The JST rendering âinnsâ seems to point to public accommodations (JST 2:7). But this term may refer to a home where Joseph and Mary seek shelter rather than a commercial establishment (see the Note on 2:7). In light of the âupper roomâ of 22:12, the chamber that the disciples are to look for is in an elevated part of the home (see the Note below).

22:12 upper room: This term (Greek anagaion) generates a good deal of comment. Some traditions identify this room with the place of the Apostlesâ meeting with 120 other followers of the Savior some six or seven weeks later to decide who will succeed the deceased traitor Judas (see Acts 1:13â26). Other traditions hold that it may be a part of the house of Mary, mother of Mark, where church members meet on at least one occasion to pray for the imprisoned Peter (see Acts 12:5, 12).36 But the word translated âupper roomâ in Acts 1:13 (Greek hyperÅon) is not the same as that in Luke 22:12. In fact, the âupper roomâ of Acts 1:13 probably points to a well known meeting locale among church members in Jerusalem. The Greek term in Acts 1:13 is the same as that found often in the Septuagint, which is used to translate Hebrew words referring to upper rooms in Godâs dwelling (see NIV âupper chambers,â Ps. 104:3, 13; LXX Ps. 103:3, 13), in the temple (see LXX 1 Chr. 28:11; 2 Chr. 3:9; Ezek. 41:7; 42:5), in palaces (see LXX 4 Kgs. 1:2; 23:12; NIV Judg. 3:20, 23â25), at the top of gates (see LXX 2 Kgs. 19:1; Jer. 20:2), and in large homes (see NIV Jer. 22:13â14). In a few passages, the term refers to what may be a small room or small apartment (see LXX 3 Kgs. 17:19, 23; 4 Kgs. 4:10â11; Dan. 6:10). But the majority of occurrences point to a large room in a large structure to which access is gained without going through the other parts of the building, usually by an outside staircase (see LXX 4 Kgs. 4:10â11; NIV Judg. 3:23â25; NIV Ezek. 41:7). Thus, the owner of such a room can sublet that part of the building to others (consult LXX 3 Kgs. 17:19, 23; NIV 2 Kgs. 4:10â11), which is apparently the situation with the âupper roomâ of Acts 1:13.

furnished: The Greek verb strÅnnumi bears the sense âto strewâ or âto spreadâ (with pillows or pads)37 and points to a banquet scene where guests recline.38 Most commentators judge that Jesus arranges for this place, but the women followers noted in 23:55â56 are more than capable of securing the spot and seeing to its basic comfort.

22:13 they went: The verb is the same that describes Judasâs action, bearing the meaning âto departâ (see the Note on 22:4). The enduring difference between the men rests in the reason why each departs from the Saviorâs presenceâJudas, with malice, to fix a plan for betrayal, Peter and John, in obedience, to see about final arrangements for a memorable feast.

found as he had said: In words almost identical to those found elsewhere (see 19:32), we grasp the idea that Jesus is in charge of matters and that his predictions are validated.39

they made ready the passover: The arrangements will include purchasing an unblemished male lamb or kid (see Ex. 12:5),40 seeing to its proper slaughter and roasting, and obtaining the other essential foods for the meal such as wine and unleavened bread.41 Again, though unmentioned, the women disciples, who take part in other ways (see 23:55â24:1), are likely involved in these activities (see the Note on 22:8).

Analysis

These verses mark a sharp contrast, one that illumines the deep rift that now opens up between Judas and the rest of the Twelve. According to prior verses, Judas maliciously steps quietly away from the fellowship of the Savior and his brethren of the Twelve and negotiates the betrayal of Jesus to plotting authorities who seek to seize him âin the absence of the multitudeâ (22:6). He now represents Satan (see 22:3; John 13:2, 27). In total contrast, Peter and John enjoy the bright light of Jesusâ full trust, illustrated when he hands over to them the arrangements for their most important and final meal together. Although they do not yet know that it is their last meal with him, Jesus is fully aware of its significance.

Because of how Luke and the other Synoptists recount events leading up to the Supper, we conclude that this meal is the Passover dinner.42 Although some challenge this obvious connection, chiefly because the roasted lamb is not mentioned in any of the accounts as being consumed during the meal,43 the symbolic central element of the feast is clearly sitting on the table when Jesus says âthis passoverâ (22:15). Certainly all of the synoptic Gospels label the Last Supper as a Passover celebration (see 22:7; Matt. 26:17â19; Mark 14:12, 14, 16). The bigger issue lies in the difference between these accounts and Johnâs Gospel. For that text clearly notes that the Last Supper falls the night before the Passover celebrationââNow before the feast of the passover,â the âsupper being endedâ (John 13:1â2; also John 19:14, 31; emphasis added). This difference, whether the Last Supper is a Passover meal or a pre-Passover occasion, forms one of the most difficult problems for students of the New Testament. To date, this difficulty defies solution. For our purposes, we adopt Lukeâs understanding that Jesus and the Twelve prepare for and participate in a Passover meal together.

Such a solution agrees with the goal of Luke, along with those of Matthew and Mark, to show that Jesus transforms the celebration of the deliverance of the Hebrew slaves from Egypt into a celebration of the deliverance of all humankind through his Atonement by adding more meaning to the wine and unleavened bread. This is certainly the pattern in the Book of Mormon.44 Johnâs Gospel, on the other hand, seeks to highlight Jesus as the Lamb of God: it omits reference to the bread and wine at the meal, and thus possible ties to a Passover meal, holding that Jesus, the Lamb, dies on the cross during the afternoon of the day of preparation, just as the paschal lambs die in the temple during the day before they are consumed in the evening Passover meals (see Ex. 12:8, 42; John 18:28; 19:14, 31, 42).45 But Lukeâs notation that âthe passover must be killedâ (22:7) may well bring readers inside this concept. For Lukeâs expression breathes the same air as Johnâs account, hinting strongly at the divine necessity of the Passover lamb being sacrificed so that Godâs people are delivered (see the Note on 22:7).

In another vein, into our line of sight emerge disciples who seem to be hiding in plain view. The first, of course, is âthe goodman of the houseâ (22:11). Every reason exists to believe that this man is a follower of the Savior who knows him by the title âthe Master.â46 It does not do to say that this title is a codeword that the homeowner will recognize from a prior contact with Jesus when he supposedly comes to arrange for a place for the Last Supper. Jesus is known widely by this title, as its frequent appearance in Lukeâs report demonstrates (see 8:49; 9:38; 10:25; 18:18; etc.; the Note on 7:40). The other disciples who emerge, I suggest, are the women whom Luke introduces earlier in his narrative (see 8:2â3). His choice to feature them early on does not mean that they end their association with the Savior soon afterward. They are still committed followers, likely with assignments; they are still in Jesusâ entourage (see 23:49, 55â56); and they are still offering support from âtheir substanceâ as Luke points out when he brings them to our notice (8:2â3). This last point, I believe, invites us to see them as unnamed participants in the preparations for the Passover meal. Their experienced and skillful hands, I judge, will be fully evident in the final adornment of the âupper roomâ and the readying of the supper. As Richard Bauckham reminds us when discussing the young man in Mark 14:51â52, âwe need not suppose that only the Twelve were at the Last Supper.â47

Incidentally, this is not the first visit of the Twelve to Jerusalem in Jesusâ company.48 To be sure, the synoptic Gospels paint this picture, leading readers to see Jesusâ last visit as his first with his disciples. But, besides the weight of the reports in the fourth Gospel that chronicle a number of visits (see John 2:13; 5:1; 7:10; 11:17â18; 12:12â13), and the hint that temple and civil authorities are looking for Jesus at the festival because of earlier tense interactions with him (see the Note on 19:47), Luke preserves a bigger clue that Peter and John already know their way around the city when they ask, âWhere wilt thou that we prepare?â (22:9). Though one scholar holds that the two are asking Jesus for directions because they do not know their way around town,49 the plain sense of their question points to their wish to know exactly the place that Jesus has in mind, and which they with evident confidence can find (see the Notes on 10:38; 13:34; 19:47; 23:5, 28).

Institution of the Sacrament(22:14â20)(Compare Matt. 26:20, 26â29; Mark 14:17, 22â25)

Translation Notes and Comments

22:14 the hour was come: This sort of expression is more at home in Johnâs Gospel (see John 2:4; 5:25, 28; 12:23, 27; 17:1; for the order of the Passover service, see the Analysis below). In other accounts, particularly Johnâs, âthe hourâ has to do with Jesusâ Atonement, as it obviously does here (see Mark 14:35, 41; John 2:4; 7:30; 8:20; 12:23, 27; 13:1; 17:1; also Matt. 26:45).50 As we have seen, Johannine language appears elsewhere in Lukeâs report (see 10:21â22; 22:53; and the Notes thereon). Lukeâs reference to âthe hourâ bestows holiness on the scene because the Passover is already underway. Significantly, the expression also points to the evening, following sundown. This time is unusual for the main meal of the day that is customarily served at midday (see the Note on 11:37).51 The Passover meal is always served in the evening (see Ex. 12:8; Deut. 16:6).52

he sat down: The Greek verb anapiptÅ means to recline although, because the room is âfurnishedâ with mats and pillows, some may be sitting (see 14:10; the Notes on 5:29; 7:36; 11:37; 14:8, 10; 17:7; 24:30).53 In support, the term translated âfurnishedâ bears the sense that pillows are carefully placed for reclining guests (see the Note on 22:12).54 Besides, later Jewish law, which is likely in effect in Jesusâ day, requires that all recline at the Passover meal to symbolize that people no longer need to be ready to flee in haste (see Ex. 12:11; Deut. 16:3).55

the twelve apostles with him: The dinner is a private affair and apparently does not include other disciples among the guests, however close they may be to the Savior, although the Galilean women followers are likely serving the meal (see the Notes on 22:8, 12â13; the Analysis on 22:7â13). Thus Luke underscores the high significance and warm intimacy of this occasion. The original reading of the narration seems to be âapostlesâ without specifying the number twelve, as shown by a number of texts, including P75, the earliest.56 In addition, the phrase âwith himâ points decidedly to the discipleship that has continued to this moment (see 22:33, 56, 59; 24:44; the Notes on 6:17; 8:1, 45; 22:28; 23:43; 24:33).57 Importantly for Passover, it is required that people gather in groups of ten or more for the celebration, a duty that Jesus and his disciples fulfill.58

22:15 with desire I have desired: Many scholars see this expression as a translation from a Hebrew or Aramaic original, termed grammatically an infinitive absolute where the verb and its object derive from the same root. Most scholars see it as language influenced by Septuagint expressions.59 Even so, if we understand the words to come from the Savior himself, originally in Hebrew or Aramaic, then they likely disclose some of the color of his discussion at the supper as he speaks in the rhythms of his native language.60

this passover: Even though a question arises whether the supper is really a Passover meal,61 this term on Jesusâ lips almost certainly means that the Passover lamb rests on the table in front of him and the Twelve. It is a Passover meal.62

with you: Typically, the father of the house takes charge of the Passover service and meal (see Ex. 13:8, âthou shalt shew thy sonâ).63 In this scene, Jesus takes the place of that father and transforms his Apostles into the leaders of the new Exodus, somewhat in the fashion of the people of the Dead Sea.64 Beyond this, the phrase has to do with enduring discipleship (see 22:33; the Notes on 6:17; 22:14, 28).65

I suffer: Jesus announces for the first time at the supper that his death is at hand (see 22:18â20, 30, 37).66 As we know, his suffering is one of the focuses not only of his prophecies from at least the time of his transfiguration (see 18:31â33; the Notes on 9:22; 12:50; 17:25) but also of his later review of events of this day and the day following (see 24:26, 46; also Acts 1:3; 3:18; 17:3; D&C 19:16â18).

22:16 I will not . . . eat: This remark does not mean that Jesus begins to fast or refuses to eat; on the contrary, the earliest account says âwhen he had suppedâ (1 Cor. 11:25; see JST Mark 14:24). The earliest manuscripts of Luke preserve a strong negative (Greek ou mÄ) that points to Jesusâ very firm intentââI say unto youâ is part of his emphasis67ânot to partake of this kind of a meal again until a later date, âuntil it be fulfilled in the kingdom of God.â68 A second aspect of Jesusâ words connect to the expected messianic banquet of the end-time to which he refers here (see 22:17â18) and later (see 22:30ââeat and drink at my tableâ).69 See the Notes on 4:3; 12:37; 13:25, 28; 14:15; 22:17â18, 30; the Analysis on 4:1â13; 9:10â17; D&C 58:8â11; 65:3.

until it be fulfilled in the kingdom: The expression is unusual and has drawn discussion to itself.70 The sense seems to be that Jesus here envisions another Passover-like meal, even more meaningful than this, at the end-time. There, he and his faithful Apostles, and others (see 13:28â29; D&C 27:5â14), will gather together to celebrate the final fulfillment of all things brought about by God.71 Importantly, the Joseph Smith Translation inserts a number of words from Jesus that highlight the prophecies about what will happen to Jesus in the next few hours: âuntil it be fulfilled which is written in the prophets concerning me. Then I will partake with you, in the kingdom of Godâ (JST 22:16; emphasis added).

22:17 he took the cup: The verb (Greek dechomai) means âto receive,â evidently signaling that Jesus receives this cup, already filled, perhaps from one of his Apostles,72 perhaps from a server. The verb that Luke chooses for âto takeâ (Greek lambanÅ) appears later in this verse and elsewhere in eating scenes (see 6:4; 9:16; 22:19; 24:30, 43).73 This cup is likely the first of four that are consumed during the service because Jesus offers a prayer of thanks over it, although some argue for the third cup.74 This cup does not have to do with the sacrament, or Eucharist. Instead, Jesusâ action here anticipates the messianic banquet (see 22:18, 30; the Note on 22:16). Further, Jesus signals that he is changing the character of the meal by asking the Twelve to share a single cup, an act of union, rather than drinking from their own cups as they usually do at the Passover.75 Of course, near the end of the meal Jesus will introduce the sacrament (see 22:19â20).

gave thanks: As the host, the Savior offers a prayer of thanks for the drink, a common practice of his day (see Matt. 15:36; Mark 8:6; John 6:11, 23; Acts 27:35).76 This custom lies at the base of our usual prayers over meals. In effect, we imitate Jesus in this prayerful action (see the Notes on 9:16; 24:30). Lukeâs verb is eucharisteÅ, which he repeats in 22:19, where it is clearly tied to the sacrament, although in other instances it is not (see also 17:16; 18:11). This word and related terms stand at the base of the Christian term âEucharist.â77

Take this: Significantly, as host, it is Jesus who offers the cup of wine. Underlying his action is a keen sense of authority. The Apostles neither have the right to take the drink from his hand if he does not offer it, nor to request it. They are the recipients of a gift, now blessed by him, which he bestows freely (see the Note on 22:19). The appearance of the same verb (Greek lambanÅ) at 9:16 when Jesus is preparing the meal for the five thousand links the two events.

divide it among yourselves: Jesus requests that the Twelve share the wine. Drinking from the same cup is an act that symbolizes and cements their sacred fellowship.78

22:18 I will not drink: Although it is possible to see Jesusâ statement here and that in 22:16 as declarations that he is not partaking of the meal,79 he rather aims his remarks at the future messianic banquet, saying that he will again share a meal with the Twelve at that event (see the Note on 22:16). Both Matthew and Mark record that Jesus utters his intent to drink new wine with the Twelve in that future setting (see Matt. 26:29; Mark 14:25), a promise that is expanded in Doctrine and Covenants 27:5â14 (see the Notes on 4:3; 13:28; 14:15; 22:16â17, 30; the Analysis on 4:1â13; 9:10â17; D&C 58:8â11; 65:3).

the fruit of the vine: This expression arises from the Jewish blessing over wine and goes back to Old Testament roots (see Deut. 22:9; Isa. 32:12).80 Thus Jesus hints that he is taking up what is old and customary and applying it to the future, in this case the messianic banquet.

until the kingdom of God shall come: Jesusâ words are close to those in 22:16. In each of these verses, he points to a special, future meal of the end-time, giving a deeper meaning to what he now will do (see the Note on 22:16).

22:19 he took bread: This bread is unleavened as required by Old Testament law (see Ex. 13:6â7; Deut. 16:3).81 Four New Testament accounts exist for what Jesus does next in instituting the sacrament or Eucharist: the earliest derives from Paul (see 1 Cor. 11:23â26), the others from the synoptic Gospels (see Matt. 26:26â29; Mark 14:22â25; Luke 22:19â20). A few passages shed light but do not repeat Jesusâ actions and accompanying words on this occasion (see John 6:51â58; Acts 20:7, 11). Among all, Luke and Paul are closest in agreement. The language points to Jesus as the main actor when he takes the bread in hand (see Matt. 26:26; Mark 14:22; 1 Cor. 11:23; also 3 Ne. 18:3). In the Passover meal, the bread represents affliction, recalling the affliction that the Hebrew slaves experience before their deliverance (see Deut. 16:3). In partaking of the bread of affliction, Jewish celebrants recall the events of the Exodus as if they are actually participating with the Hebrew slaves, joining in their flight from Egypt. This concept adds a dimension of immediate association with that earlier generation under Moses.82 Similarly, what Jesus does is to serve as a reminder to future worshipers that participation in the Eucharist is to be as if they are sitting in Jesusâ presence at this very moment. As the Joseph Smith Translation makes clear in Markâs record: âas oft as ye do this ye will remember this hour that I was with youâ (JST Mark 14:21, 24).83 Paulâs report hints strongly at this directive when he quotes Jesusâ saying about the wine, âthis do ye, as oft as ye drink it, in remembrance of meâ (1 Cor. 11:25). In the eyewitness account, presumably not at a Passover celebration, Jesus introduces the sacrament to his New World hearers and pointedly says that he has âshown [his body] unto [them]â (3 Ne. 18:7). Such a notice nods at the concept that these worshipers and their spiritual heirs will recall this moment with the Savior. Importantly, in Lukeâs record Jesus transforms the bread from a remembrance of affliction in bondage to one that points to himself (1) as the messianic deliverer from sin,84 (2) as the bringer of the kingdom of God in the end-time, and (3) as the one who suffers vicariously for all (see 22:16, 18).85

gave thanks, and brake: The order of Jesusâ actions is the same as in Matthew 26:26, Mark 14:22, and 1 Corinthians 11:24. The only eyewitness accounts of the Risen Jesusâ actions in instituting the sacrament reverse the order: âbrake and blessedâ (3 Ne. 18:3; 20:3). The Joseph Smith Translation of Matthewâs report reverses his order, harmonizing it with the eyewitness reports: âbrake [the bread], and blessed itâ (JST Matt. 26:22).

gave unto them: Jesus continues to play the host. Again, his simple action brims with authority, the authority to transform the Passover meal into one that memorializes himself as deliverer, replacing Moses.86 Such authority manifests itself most clearly in the eyewitness reports when the Risen Savior, with authority, âcommanded [the twelve disciples] that they should give unto the multitudeâ (3 Ne. 18:4; 20:4). In the New Testament setting, Jesus hands the broken bread to one of the Apostles and it is then passed from hand to hand around the table.87

This is my body: We now engage the Words of Institution, as they are known, the words that frame the meaning of Jesusâ actions. They appear here and in verse 20. As we might expect, the various New Testament accounts are harmonized by later scribes, leading to no fewer than five versions of Jesusâ words in Lukeâs Gospel and creating what Fitzmyer calls âthe most notorious [textual problem] in the entire Gospel.â88 Remarkably, the Joseph Smith Translation makes only one adjustment in verses 19 and 20 as they appear in the King James text, omitting the italicized word âitâ in verse 19 following the verb âbrakeâ; no change appears in the account of 1 Corinthians 11:23â26. A series of changes occur in the reports of Matthew and Mark, leaving the impression that Lukeâs version is fully accurate, as far as it goes. That said, the JST additions to Matthew and Mark say much about how we are to understand this significant scene. First, the additional words in JST Matthew are spare and clear: âthis is in remembrance of my body which I give a ransom for youâ (JST Matt. 26:22; emphasis added). Next, among other items, the JST language in Mark introduces the intriguing infinitive âto doâ that is missing in the other accounts except one of the eyewitness reports: âthis is for you to do in remembrance of my bodyâ (JST Mark 14:21; emphasis added). Similarly, according to the eyewitness report, in speaking of blessing and distributing the bread the Risen Savior declares, âthis shall ye always observe to doâ (3 Ne. 18:6). What does the verb âto doâ mean in the context of the sacrament? In the first place, it points to an authorized person who âshall break bread and bless it and give it unto the people of [the Saviorâs] church . . . even as I [the Savior] have doneâ (3 Ne. 18:5â6), precisely mirroring Jesusâ actions. Second, it concerns a âremembrance of [Jesusâ] bodyâ that all worshipers engage in, replacing the remembrance of the Exodus (JST Matt. 26:22; JST Mark 14:21; 3 Ne. 18:7). Depending on the source, his body âis given for youâ (Luke 22:19), âis broken for youâ (only certain manuscripts of 1 Cor. 11:24; others read âis given for youâ or âis for youâ), is âa ransom for youâ (JST Matt. 26:22), and, in the case of the Risen Jesusâ appearance in the New World, his bodyâwith its scarsâis âshown unto youâ (3 Ne. 18:7). All of these senses coalesce under the title âremembranceâ and point to a broad meaning of remembering not only Jesusâ actions at the Last Supper but also his body that is crucified and resurrected (see D&C 20:75ââin the remembrance of the Lord Jesusâ).89 In this connection, the JST adds intriguing words from Jesus at this moment: âfor as oft as ye do this ye will remember this hour that I was with youâ (JST Mark 14:22; see the Note on 22:20; the Analysis on 22:14â20). Third, this act of remembering becomes a âtestimony unto the Fatherâ which allows worshipers to âhave [the Lordâs] Spirit to be with [them]â (3 Ne. 18:7; 20:9). Fourth, the verb âto doâ bears the sense of âfulfilling [the Saviorâs] commandmentsâ and being âwilling to do that which [he has] commanded youâ (3 Ne. 18:10).

body: The Greek term sÅma can point both to the self, meaning that Jesus is referring to himself, and to the flesh, meaning that he emphasizes the mortal part of himself that he shares with all of us (see 3:6), which will now become a sacrificial offering.90

given for you: In the explanation of sacrifice, God makes it clear that he is the one who gives the gift of allowing us âupon the altar to make an atonementâ (Lev. 17:11), thus conferring a divine acceptance onto our gift.91 Here, it is Jesus who gives himself. In another vein, the vicarious nature of Jesusâ death rises into view: the sense behind the verb âto giveâ (Greek didÅmi) with the preposition hyper is to give oneâs life.92 This concept receives support in Matthew and Mark: âto give his life a ransom for manyâ (Matt. 20:28; Mark 10:45). Perhaps influenced by these passages, the Joseph Smith Translation adds to Matthewâs record the following: âmy body which I give a ransom for youâ (JST Matt. 26:22; also John 6:51; emphasis added).

do: Here the Greek verb poieÅ, replicated in 1 Corinthians 11:24 (see 3 Ne. 18:7), focuses on the Twelve repeating exactly what Jesus does. According to the eyewitness report, those who officiate in the sacramental ordinance are âto doâ âeven as I [the Savior] have done, even as I have broken bread and blessed it and given it unto you [the twelve disciples]. . . . I have set an example for youâ (3 Ne. 18:6, 16). Hence, Jesusâ command refers to both administering the sacrament with authority, exactly as he does, and partaking with faith (see John 6:53â58; 3 Ne. 18:13, 24; 20:8â9).93

in remembrance of me: This phrase appears also in 1 Corinthians 11:24, thus preserving an early memory of Jesusâ words. In minor contrast, the Joseph Smith Translation of Matthew and Mark (as well as the eyewitness account) repeats a fuller version of these words: âin remembrance of my bodyâ (JST Matt. 26:22; JST Mark 14:21; 3 Ne. 18:7). The difference in meaning is modest but receives emphasis in another expression in modern scripture: âbread and wineâthe emblems of the flesh and blood of Christâ (D&C 20:40). The term translated âremembranceâ (Greek anamnÄsis) carries three distinct senses that flow together in the sacramental service: a recollection âin the consciousness,â a remembrance âby word,â and a recollection âby act.â94 This last, of course, is performed by lifting the bread and wine (or water) to oneâs mouth. By this simple act, a participant ties a symbolic ritual with a remembrance of the Apostlesâ experience with Jesus that night.

22:20 the cup after supper: According to later Jewish tradition, two cups of wine follow the Passover meal, âwhen he had suppedâ (1 Cor. 11:25), one with the blessing over the meal and the other with the singing of the Hallel songs which, for Passover, consists of Psalms 113â18.95 The cup in question is likely the third cup that is always paired with a blessing.96 The supper itself separates the two parts of the sacramental ordinance, the bread coming at its beginning and the cup at its end. According to John, the end of the supper is further marked by Jesus washing the feet of the Twelve, the departure of Judas, and a series of special teachings (see John 13:2â18:1).

This cup: In both Luke and Paul, the initial emphasis falls on the cup (see 1 Cor. 11:25). In the other accounts, Jesus draws attention to the wine in the cup, which figuratively points to his blood, an observation strengthened in the JST: âthis is in remembrance of my bloodâ (JST Matt. 26:24; JST Mark 14:23; also 3 Ne. 18:11).

the new testament: The more accurate translation is âthe new covenantâ (Greek diathÄkÄ). Naturally, the term âcovenantâ gathers to itself all the meanings that the Old Testament can pour into this term, including Godâs covenants with both Abraham and with the newly freed Israelite slaves. But more than this, Jesusâ language alludes to Godâs promise to the prophet Jeremiah:97 âI will make a new covenant with the house of Israel . . . Not according to the covenant that I made with their fathers . . . I will put my law in their inward parts . . . and will be their God, and they shall be my people. . . . For they shall all know me . . . and I will remember their sin no moreâ (Jer. 31:31â34).

in my blood: As Paulâs record agrees, the new covenant will gain its life from Jesusâ blood, not from the blood of animals sacrificed on the templeâs altar (see 1 Cor. 11:25; Heb. 9:11â28). This concept receives striking visual power from the red color of the wine that sits on the table in front of Jesus and the Apostles.98 These men are fully aware of biblical passages that underline blood as a key of life and of covenant making: âthe life of the flesh is in the blood: and I [God] have given it to you upon the altar to make an atonement for your soulsâ (Lev. 17:11, 14).99 In addition, âBehold the blood of the covenant, which the Lord hath made with youâ (Ex. 24:8).

shed for you: This expression, aimed intimately and personally at the Twelve (see also 1 Cor. 11:24â25), is broadened to the expression âshed for manyâ in Matthew 26:28 and Mark 14:24. In these passages, the sense of the âmanyâ may include all people (see 1 Tim. 2:6; Heb. 2:9).100 At this point in Markâs rehearsal, the Joseph Smith Translation adds the following words from Jesus: âAnd as oft as ye do this ordinance, ye will remember me in this hour that I was with you and drank with you of this cup, even the last time in my ministryâ (JST Mark 14:24; see the Note on 22:19). This added saying not only captures the essence of the Passoverâs immediacy in the lives of participants, an aspect missing from all the Gospel reports (see the Analysis below), but also specifies that on this night Jesus drinks in fellowship with his beloved Apostles. He is not fasting.

Analysis

In these verses we stand on holy ground. Here we are allowed into the room where, in simple but powerful words and actions, the Savior is teaching his beloved and trusted Apostles about the meaning of his death and how they can remember it. While he and they recline in this intimate setting, we can almost smell the freshly prepared foods while we overhear his measured words in his attempt to help his followers grasp for the first time how he wants them to recollect the events that will soon burst upon him and them. In a quiet, worshipful location, far from the turbulence that will characterize the next few hours, he asks them to break bread and pour wine and remember in coming days his willingness to go to his death for them and others. His is the ultimate vicarious giftââmy body which is given for you . . . my blood, which is shed for youâ (22:19â20).

To be sure, Luke does not preserve the whole scene as it unfolds, as the adjustments in the Joseph Smith Translation of Matthew and Mark illustrate. But because the JST makes few changes in Lukeâs report, we can reasonably say that his account is accurate as far as it goes,101 almost totally agreeing with what comes down to the Apostle Paul. Unlike others, who struggle to make sense of the New Testament accounts of this sceneâaccounts that exhibit differences among themselves (see Matt. 26:26â29; Mark 14:22â25; 1 Cor. 11:23â26)âLatter-day Saints enjoy the illumination that comes not only from the JST but, perhaps more importantly, from the eyewitness accounts in the New World (see 3 Ne. 18:1â14; 20:3â9). Although the occasion for the eyewitness reports likely does not fall on a Passover, the common rope that binds all accounts together is the Saviorâs institution of the sacrament or Eucharist.

As in the story of the feeding of the five thousand, Jesus presides as host. A connection clearly exists between the two accounts. Jesus takes charge; he blesses the five loaves and breaks them; he then distributes them through the Twelve (see the Note on 9:16). The part of the story that stands out is that all are filled by this unpretentious meal (see 9:17). Likewise, in instituting the sacrament among his New World worshipers, all eat and are filled (see 3 Ne. 18:5; 20:8â9). Lukeâs report of the Last Supper, of course, assumes that everyone in the room eats until filled. To be sure, the parallels between the stories of the five thousand and the Last Supper are not completely tidy. Luke pens the verb translated âto blessâ when writing about Jesusâ actions for the five thousand (Greek eulogeÅ) and âto give thanksâ (Greek eucharisteÅ) when describing Jesusâ efforts with the Twelve (see 9:16; 22:19). But in a broad sense the two verbs serve as synonyms, as we see in Mark 6:41 and 8:6.102

The fourth Gospel portrays the Last Supper as a pre-Passover meal: âbefore the feast of the passover,â the âsupper being endedâ (John 13:1â2). Contrarily, the Last Supper in the synoptic Gospels forms a Passover meal.103 As Jesus reclines with his associates, he pointedly refers to âthis passover,â fixing on the roasted lamb that sits on the table in front of them (22:15). As with many differences between the Gospel accounts, this one defies explanation. Johnâs chronology fits with his view that Jesus dies as the Passover lambs die (see John 19:14, 31, 42).104 But stepping beyond this observation leads us into a world of spiraling speculation.105

The Passover meal in Jesusâ day is already ritualized. The teaching, blessings and supper proceed in a fixed, orderly way.106 At the gathering for the celebration, the father or paterfamilias takes the lead. He pronounces two blessings, one on the festival itself, called the qiddush, and the second over the first cup of wine, as Jesus does (see 22:17), which is then drunk. The meal is served but not yet eaten. It consists of unleavened bread, stewed or pureed fruit, bitter herbs, and roasted lamb. In response to a sonâs rehearsed question about the special nature of this night, the father teaches everyone about the Exodus from Egypt with reference to the foods set before them, for example, the bitter herbs representing the bitter bondage. In all, the fatherâs words carry the celebrants back to the Exodus as if they are now participants. Those gathered then sing the first of the Hallel psalms, Psalm 113, and then drink the second cup of wine. After the father utters a blessing over the bread, which role Jesus performs (see 22:19), everyone enjoys the meal. At the end of the supper, âwhen he had suppedâ (1 Cor. 11:25), the father blesses the third cup, called the cup of thanksgiving which, in Lukeâs report, becomes the cup of âthe new testament in [Jesusâ] bloodâ (22:20). Participants then sing the last part of the Hallel, Psalms 114â18; the Apostles evidently sing these psalms, as retold in other sources: âwhen they had sung an hymn, they went out into the mount of Olivesâ (Matt. 26:30; Mark 14:26). Not incidentally, the line sung by the multitudes that accompany Jesus into the city lies in this extended song: âBlessed be he that cometh in the name of the Lordâ (Ps. 118:26; see the Note on 19:38).

This supper, of course, Jesus transforms from a Passover celebration into a memorial of his suffering and death, of his Atonement. As we have seen, Jesus alters the meaning of the bread of affliction, which is broken and served at the beginning of the meal, by pointing it toward himself (see Deut. 16:3; the Note on 22:19). His body, his mortal self, he surrenders as an offering. Partaking of the bread is done âin remembrance of meâ (22:19; 1 Cor. 11:24) or, more specifically, âin remembrance of my body,â as repeated in the Joseph Smith Translation of Matthew and Mark, as well as in the eyewitness account (JST Matt. 26:22; JST Mark 14:21; 3 Ne. 18:7). But Jesus retains the commingling sense attached to the Passover that participants stand shoulder to shoulder with the fleeing Hebrew slaves. For the Joseph Smith Translation adds to Markâs record: âas oft as ye do this ye will remember this hour that I was with you . . . even the last time in my ministryâ (JST Mark 14:21, 24). Future worshipers are to memorialize the sacrament as if they are sitting in Jesusâ presence at that very moment.107

Similarly, Jesus changes the meaning of the third cup of wine which is served after the meal. Instead of presenting it with the traditional blessing or benediction to conclude the supper,108 Jesus transforms this cup of wine into a remembrance: it now represents âthe new testamentâ or new covenant âin [his] bloodâ (22:20). This new covenant replaces the old one offered through Moses (see Ex. 24:3â8; Jer. 31:31â34) and is made effective not by sacrifices on the templeâs altar but by Jesusâ âblood . . . which is shed for as many as shall believe on [his] nameâ (JST Matt. 26:24). Jesus thus places his atoning sacrifice figuratively yet firmly in front of his Apostles.

Translation Notes and Comments

22:21 But, behold: By introducing this adversative before announcing the betrayer, Jesus excludes Judas from the new covenant âin [Jesusâ] blood, which is shed for you [the Twelve]â (22:20). Judas will no longer be a part of the community of believers and will be unable to receive the blessings of the Atonement.109

the hand: In this graphic detail, Jesus nods to the dread reality of what is coming toward himself. Hands, of course, are the instruments for doing good as well as doing evil. Judasâs hand now becomes an extension of the hands of those who seek Jesusâ life (see 20:19).

him that betrayeth me: Perhaps significantly, Jesus does not mention Judasâs name, though he knows who will betray him,110 much as Luke will not say that Judas actually kisses Jesus, thus creating a subtle but purposeful distance between the Savior and his betrayer (see 22:47â48). One of the most odious actions has to do with turning against a person with whom we share a meal (see Ps. 41:9).111 Judasâs act will break the sacred fellowship of the Twelve.

the table: This table becomes the place of contrasts where Jesus unfolds the meaning of his Atonement and where the betrayer shares a meal with his fellow members of the Twelve. In the future, only those worthy to gather at the Saviorâs table will be allowed (see the Notes on 13:25, 28; 22:30).

22:22 the Son of man goeth: In less than an hour, Jesus will go to Gethsemane, where he will begin the suffering that will effect the Atonement. Although the path is fixed, as he says next, he goes willingly and not as a captive of some invisible hand. In this instance, Jesus terms himself as âthe Son of man,â just as he does elsewhere in connection with his ministry (see 6:5, 22; 7:34; 9:22, 26; 11:30; 12:8, 10, 40; etc.; the Note on 5:24).

determined: The participle of the Greek verb horizÅ bears the sense of âplanned, decreedâ in this context. In other passages that apply directly to Jesus himself, the meaning is âappointed, ordainedâ (see Acts 10:42; 17:31; Rom. 1:4; also the Notes on 3:2 and 4:18). It is worth adding that no sense of determinism attaches to these passages.112

woe unto that man: The warning, which Judas hears, will eventually involve him as a son of perdition (see John 17:12; 3 Ne. 27:32; 29:7; also D&C 76:31â38, 43â48), not counting his unfortunate death (see Matt. 27:5; Acts 1:18).

22:23 they began to enquire among themselves: This sudden curiosity that floods across the eleven Apostles is documented more completely elsewhere (see Matt. 26:21â25; Mark 14:18â21; John 13:21â30). Luke is spare in his detail, even omitting âit had been good for that man [Judas] if he had not been bornâ (Matt. 26:24; Mark 14:21). As the Greek verb syzÄteÅ indicates, meaning âto discussâ or âto dispute, to debate,â they enter into an intense discussion.113

Analysis

Without naming him, the Savior now brings Judas front and center, but only for a very brief moment. What Judas is about to do does not take over the story.114 Instead, his betrayal fits within the larger account that focuses on what Jesus does and will experience. To be sure, Judasâs act triggers everything else that follows. Curiously, we do not learn whether Judas is surprised by Jesusâ prescient remark about a betrayer. But by now he knows that Jesus possesses unusual powers to perceive the unseen (see the Notes on 5:22; 6:8; 11:17; 20:23; 21:3). Only Matthew writes that Judas keeps up the false faÃ§adeâin the midst of the swirling conversation, Judas is reported to ask, âMaster, is it I?â to which Jesus responds, âThou has saidâ (Matt. 26:25). Perhaps oddly, Luke does not write of Judasâs departure from the room to meet authorities; only John does (see John 13:26â30; the Note on 22:24).
