Translation Notes and Comments

22:54 took . . . and led . . . and brought: As the verbs indicate, the authorities complete the arrest, finally securing Jesus as their prisoner, but not until Jesus finishes his short speech (see 22:52â53). This act shows that he is in charge, though they do not know it.233 It is his choice, not theirs: he âyieldeth himself . . . into the hands of wicked menâ (1 Ne. 19:10). The verb translated âtookâ (Greek syllambanÅ) means âto arrest.â234 The verb translated âledâ (Greek agÅ) is the same as we find in the Septuagint rendering of Isaiah 53:7â8 (âhe was led as a sheep . . . he was led to deathâ),235 although the past tenses differ, the first aorist in Luke and second aorist in Isaiah (see the Note on 23:1).

the high priestâs house: According to John 18:13 and 18:24, the arresting party âled [Jesus] away to Annas firstâ and then âAnnas sent [Jesus] bound unto Caiaphas the high priest.â The locations are most probably on the upper west side of the city where other high priests and dignitaries make their homes.236 Two further points emerge. (1) Annas serves as high priest from AD 6 to 15 and Caiaphas from AD 18 to 36. Thus, Caiaphas is high priest at the time of Jesusâ execution (see the Note on 3:2).237 (2) Because Annas is father-in-law to Caiaphas, as John 18:13 affirms, then Jesus likely remains in the same house during the entire hearing, with Annas and his immediate family inhabiting the first floor of the house and Caiaphas and his immediate family inhabiting another floor of the same building, just as we see in the construction of ancient homes238 and in the Middle East today. We find no reason to think of separate buildings for the two residences.

Peter followed afar off: We assume that Peter can see where the crowd is taking Jesus because the full Passover moon is shining and, as John writes, the crowd comes âwith lanterns and torchesâ (John 18:3). Hence, the torchlight reflects off walls and shows Peter the crowdâs location and direction. Presumably, there are no other lights. Peterâs appearance by himself in Lukeâs narrative forms a literary inclusio that ties back to his first appearance (see the Notes on 5:3 and 24:12).239

22:55 when they had kindled: The subject âtheyâ of this clause remains unknown. Perhaps it is the same as in the prior verse: Luke has in mind members of the arresting band. With this verse, Lukeâs narrative shifts away from Jesus and focuses solely on Peter, thus differing from Matthew and Mark, who sandwich Peterâs denial between notices of the early morning hearing (see Matt. 26:59â68; 27:1; Mark 14:55â65; 15:1). The hearing for Jesus, of course, is about to begin in the house as Lukeâs notices of time indicate (see 22:58, 59). According to John, that night âwas coldâ (John 18:18).

the hall: Properly, an outdoor courtyard (Greek aulÄ).240 Such walled courtyards form part of ancient homes. Because the high priest likely resides on the upper west side of the city, the nicer part of town,241 the multistoried home (see the Note on 22:54) rests on a slope and the courtyard evidently sits at the bottom level of the structure.

Peter sat down among them: Peter now joins a group that is completely different from the group with whom he reclines and dines the prior evening. Rather than welcoming him, members of this group become suspicious of him, as he might expect from strangers.

22:56 a certain maid: The young woman is a servant (Greek paidiskÄ).242 Unlike the women of the household, who usually do not venture into the city,243 this young lady walks about and picks up news as she runs errands. What she will soon say demonstrates that she moves freely through the city.

This man: As is fitting in her society, the servant maiden does not address Peter directly because he is a man and she is not to talk to a strange man.244 Rather, she speaks to no one in particular about him.

with him: As in other instances, this prepositional phrase points directly at discipleship (see 8:1, 38; 22:59; 23:43; 24:44; Matt. 26:38, 40; Mark 3:14; the Notes on 6:17; 8:45; 22:28).245

22:57 he denied: Lukeâs verb (Greek arneomai) picks up Jesusâ words âhe that denieth me before men shall be denied before the angels of Godâ (12:9), thus bringing Peter potentially under divine condemnation as does the disciplesâ earlier denial (see the Note on 12:9; the Analysis below; also D&C 101:5). Moreover, he becomes an indirect participant in the Jewish authoritiesâ rejection of Jesus when they accuse and effectively deny him before Pilate (see 23:2).246 Incidentally, this movement from the general group of disciples in JST 12:9â11 to Peter in this passage fits a pattern found elsewhere in Luke and the other Gospels, wherein Peter and his fellow disciples are either contrasted or stand together.247

Woman, I know him not: Lukeâs account of Peterâs three denials is more measured in tone than in the Gospel of Matthew, which recounts Peter uttering an oath to affirm the truth of his words (see Matt. 26:72).

22:58 after a little while: Time passes, allowing Peter not only to reconsider what he says in his first denial but also to recall what Jesus teaches about those who deny him and, in contrast, those who receive divine aid in difficult situations, as the one he now faces (see 9:26; 21:14â15).248

another: This person is a man, as the masculine noun shows (Greek heteros). This notation differs from Matthew and Mark, who write of a second servant girl accusing Peter (see Matt. 26:71; Mark 14:69), underlining the observation that Lukeâs source is independent of these Gospels.

of them: The man has in mind Peterâs associates, that is, the Apostles and other disciplesâpeople whom he dislikes. Remarkably, this man does not know that Peter, who draws his sword at the arrest, sits next to him (see John 18:10). In denying his association with the Apostles, Peter extends the effects of his denials to his beloved fellow believers.

22:59 one hour after: More time passes. As in the prior verse, Luke gives space both for the hasty gathering for the hearing of Jesus and for Peter to rethink what he is saying to these strangers.

with him: As in 22:56 and elsewhere, this prepositional phrase has to do with the discipleship of Peter and his associates (see 8:1, 38; 24:44; the Notes on 8:45; 22:28).249

Galilaean: We presume with Matthew that Peterâs distinct Galilean speech indicates his origin (see Matt. 26:73). In contrast, Pilate, because his interview with Jesus is evidently conducted in Greek (see the Notes on 2:39; 23:3), can only learn that Jesus is a Galilean from his accusers (see 23:5â6).

22:60 Peter said: Lukeâs quotation of Peterâs third denial omits the reference to Peterâs cursing, which Matthew and Mark preserve (see Matt. 26:74; Mark 14:71). What is Lukeâs motivation for this omission? Might he not be exercising his writerâs prerogative to omit a detail that would show the chief Apostle in a bad light?250

the cock crew: Two matters arise. First, roosters crow very early in the morning, which does not mesh well with the observation that most of the night passes by the time of the arrest (see the Note on 22:46).251 Hence, the arresting band may arrive a little earlier, say, between four and five oâclock, about the time of the shofar blowing that occurs after a cock crows.252 Second, since according to later Jewish law âit was forbidden to . . . rear chickens in Jerusalemâ to prevent impurity from entering the city,253 it is important that Peter and others, including priests in the temple, can hear a rooster crowing. If the Gospel sources are correctâthere is no reason to doubt themâwe can suggest that the house of Annas stands close enough to the city wall, and to a familyâs chicken roost which lies outside the wall, so that the rooster can easily be heard. This is perhaps the only hint in the Gospels about the location of the high priestâs home.

22:61 the Lord turned: This detail, as those in 7:9 and 22:41, points to an eyewitness. Compare also John 12:3 (âthe house was filled with the odourâ); see 9:55; 10:23; 14:25; 18:40; 19:3; 22:41; 23:28; John 8:6â8; and the Notes on 7:9, 44. The title âLordâ (Greek kyrios), of course, points to Jehovah, as we see elsewhere (see the Notes on 2:11; 5:8; 7:13, 31).254

looked upon Peter: Where might Jesus be standing at this moment so that Peter is in his sight-line? Evidently, the room where the early morning hearing is to take place, likely on an upper level of âthe high priestâs houseâ (22:54; see the Note thereon), also overlooks the courtyard where Peter and the others are warming themselves.

Peter remembered: Although we do not know how much Peter in this moment recalls of Jesusâ distant words about denying him (see 12:9), he now comes to full memory of Jesusâ recent prediction in response to his haughty boast (see 22:33â34).

the word: The term translated âwordâ (Greek rhÄma) has to do with a specific message, this one to Peter, rather than the whole gospel (see 2:17, 29, 50, 51; 4:4; 7:1; etc.; the Note on 3:2).255

22:62 went out: Peter now escapes the cluster of Jesusâ enemies, retreating to a place where he is alone.

Peter . . . wept bitterly: Peter undergoes a forced humility after boasting that he will never abandon Jesus (see 22:33; the Note on 22:32). Although Peterâs healing from his denials begins with his repentance, initiated by Jesus looking at him (see 22:61),256 he will apparently not be fully healed emotionally and spiritually until his meetings with the Savior, first by himself after the resurrection (see 24:34; also 1 Cor. 15:5) and then on the shore of the Sea of Galilee, where Jesus confidently hands to Peter the responsibility of caring for the church in his absence (see John 21:15â17).

Analysis

For the first time since chapter 5, Peter glides from behind the curtains of either anonymity, or a shared spotlight with the Twelve, and stands before our eyesâalone. He does not share the spotlight with his brother Andrew who is present in the earlier scene, although, notably, his brother remains unnamed (see the Notes on 5:5â6). He does not stand with his fishing partners, James and John, as he does so often. Instead, Peter sits with people who wish his master harm, basically leaving him alone; alone he warms himself by a fire kindled by foes; alone he rolls thoughts through his head before he denies the Savior; alone he weighs the frightful prospects for his divine friend who is now in custody; alone he chases conflicting memories about Judas, his fellow Apostle and betrayer; alone he forms the focus of all our gaze. Everyone else in this scene remains nameless, almost faceless, except for gender. And except for Peter.

Even though Luke marks Peterâs devastating experience by noting the passage of time, we must not think that the approaching hearing for Jesus requires a long time. Rather, these notationsââafter a little whileâ and âabout the space of one hour afterâ (22:58, 59)âconvey a different message, at least for Peterâs situation. Between Peterâs denials, he has time to think of what he says. This observation adds texture and depth to his final act in this scene: he âwept bitterlyâ (22:62). Following his experience, he finds healing in his soul only in the solo visit of the Risen Savior to him, making him the chief witness of the resurrection (see 24:34; 1 Cor. 15:5), and in Jesusâ restoration of him to his prior station of leadership when in Galilee (see John 21:15â17).

These conclusions stand against the view that, in his denials, Peter is following Jesusâ instructions to deny him so that he will survive unharmed to lead the church. Such a view does not take account of Peter sitting in the presence of his accusers as long as he does. To be sure, we can read the future tense âthou shalt deny meâ as an imperative (22:34; also Matt. 26:34; Mark 14:30).257 But a characteristic of the Gospel storiesâthe disciples acting or speaking as a group in relative anonymity, such as their shared denial of Jesus in JST 12:9â11, and then Peter vigorously emerging in a later scene; here denying the Savior after Jesusâ prophecy that he will do so and Peterâs protest that he will not258âlends weight to the observation that Peter genuinely denies his acquaintance with Jesus. Moreover, the overall character of Lukeâs report requires that we see Peter genuinely denying his acquaintance with Jesus in the face of accusations from adversaries.259

The notes about the passage of time, of course, say something about how long Peter sits in the courtyard and how long Jesus waits for his accusers. Both because of the added notices in the Joseph Smith Translation about how long the Apostles sleep in Gethsemane (see the Note on 22:46; see also JST Matt. 26:43; JST Mark 14:47), and because of Lukeâs notation of the coming of the morning before the hearing for Jesus (see 22:66), all appears to occur in the early morning, sometime around five oâclock, and does not take up more than an hour or so.260

For his act, of course, Peter reaps a certain level of guilt. But we are not to allow this fact to become the controlling sentiment in our view of this man. On this whole scene, Elder Spencer W. Kimball, at the time the acting President of the Council of the Twelve Apostles, offers the following: âRemember that Peter never denied the divinity of Christ. He only denied his association or acquaintance with the Christ, which is quite a different matter.â Further, âI do not pretend to know what Peterâs mental reactions were nor what compelled him to say what he did that terrible night. But in light of his proven bravery, courage, great devotion, and limitless love for the Master, could we not give him the benefit of the doubt and at least forgive him as his Savior seems to have done so fully[?].â261 Said another way, Peterâs denial âis a story of personal transformation through failure, self-recognition and restoration . . . a dramatic example of the encounter with the meaning of the cross that every . . . disciple must undergo.â262

Lukeâs frame for the story of Peterâs denials differs from that of Mark and Matthew. These two evangelists place their reports between notices of the nighttime hearing in front of Jewish authorities (see Matt. 26:59â68; 27:1; Mark 14:55â65; 15:1). This observation raises the question whether Luke depends on Mark for his narrative. Although many commentators see Luke borrowing and adjusting Markâs presentation, perhaps adding material from another source,263 Lukeâs sketch differs in significant ways, leading us to see his record as standing independent. For example, on the larger canvas, Lukeâs account follows Jesus then Peter to the high priest, as do Matthew and Mark. But Lukeâs attention then focuses exclusively on Peterâs denial before introducing elements of the early morning hearing for Jesus. The other Gospels, instead, deal with a nighttime hearing before raising Peterâs denials to view, a complete reversal in arrangement. In the judgment of a Jewish scholar, âthe sequence of events . . . given in Luke makes sense, while in Mark (and Matthew) the description is at the very least strange and confused.â264 Even so, we see Peterâs recollection standing behind each of these accounts.265

In smaller details, Lukeâs record exhibits further differences. For example, in Matthew and Mark the hearing before Jewish authorities occurs at night followed by the trek to Pilate when morning arrives (see Matt. 27:1â2; Mark 15:1). According to Luke, the authorities gather in the morning to decide what to do with Jesus, not at night as in the other Gospels. Lukeâs is likely the more accurate report (see 22:66).266 Moreover, and perhaps surprisingly, Luke omits the scene that features false witnesses whom the authorities assemble to accuse Jesus (see Matt. 26:59â61; Mark 14:55â59). Furthermore, Luke holds that Peterâs second accuser is a man, not a woman as in other accounts (see 22:58; Matt. 26:71; Mark 14:69). In addition, Luke preserves two indicators of passing time whereas the others repeat only one (see 22:58, 59; Matt. 26:73; Mark 14:70). The last difference that we note, although there are others, is the most striking. Only Luke preserves the record that Jesus looks at Peter after his third denial (see 22:61).267 Conclusion? Lukeâs report rests at least partly on an independent source or witness.268

The Hearing before Jewish Authorities(22:63â71)(Compare Matt. 26:59â68; Mark 14:55â65)

66 And as the day began, the elders of the people, the chief priests, and the scribes were gathered together, and they led him to their council, 67 saying, âIf you are the Christ, tell us.â But he said to them, âIf I were to tell you, you would certainly not believe. 68 And if I were to ask, you would certainly not answer. 69 But from this moment, the Son of Man will be seated on the right hand of the power of God.â 70 And all the men said, âAre you then the Son of God?â And he said to them, âYou say that I am.â 71 And they said, âWhat need do we have of further testimony? For we ourselves have heard from his own mouth!â

[/column2] [/column]

Translation Notes and Comments

22:63 the men: Inside the house, in contrast to the scene in the courtyard (see 22:55â56), men only are present. Luke does not identify them as temple police but implies such (see 22:52), although they may be servants of the high priest.269 According to Jesusâ prophecy, Gentiles or Roman soldiers will participate (see 18:32). But their participation occurs later in the morning. The episodes in which they appear are tied together by the verb empaizÅ, âto mockâ (see 23:11, 36).270

Jesus: The name does not appear in the manuscripts. Instead, the simple pronoun âhimâ occurs (Greek auton) whose natural grammatical referent is Peter in the prior verse.271 Hence, the King James translators supply the name Jesus for clarity.

mocked him, and smote: After forcing the bound Jesus to hike up to the high priestâs residence following his crushing experience in Gethsemane, certain men in the arresting party openly show their brimming frustration at not capturing him earlier in the week by turning to physical violence, as Jesus predicts (see 18:32; also 23:11). The main verb, âto mockâ (Greek empaizÅ),272 stands in the imperfect, thus conveying the sense that the indignities go on repeatedly.273

22:64 blindfolded him: With a twist of irony, those who blindfold Jesus are themselves spiritually blind.274 It may be that this act forms one step in a cruel game that gentile servants of the high priest play with the prisoner to amuse themselves while they wait for council members to gather.275

they struck him on the face: This expression is missing from many of the early manuscripts, including the earliest from about AD 200, P75.

Prophesy, who is it that smote thee?: The authorities and their henchmen treat Jesus as a false prophet, not like those who welcome him as a king (see 19:38),276 at least not in this scene (see 23:2, 37). Their words raise the question, What do these people believe about the nature of prophecy? It appears that prophecy in this context has to do with seeing or sensing what one cannot see or sense by natural means (see John 4:19). In addition, they seem to presume that such a prophet will know notable details, including the identity of individuals by name, even though these persons may not otherwise be known to a person they call âprophetâ (see 7:39 and the Note thereon).277 This concept of prophecy may be a part of the same cloth that 23:35, 37 are part of, and may hint at the influence of magic. But if the question of the guards arises from a brutal game, the force of the question may be something like, âGuess who hit you.â278

22:65 blasphemously spake they: This line represents Lukeâs judgment about the disrespectful and uncouth language of the authorities (see the Note on 23:39), whereas in the other accounts, in a reversal, the authorities accuse Jesus of blasphemy (see Matt. 26:65; Mark 14:64). Because the verb âto speakâ is in the imperfect tense, it is apparent that the council members utter blasphemous words often and continuously.279

22:66 as soon as it was day: The sun rises. According to Luke, the hearing that follows occurs during this fateful day, not at night (see Matt. 27:1; Mark 15:1).

the elders . . . the chief priests and the scribes: This list does not match exactly Lukeâs earlier notice (see 22:52). Missing are the âcaptains of the temple,â the representatives of the temple police force. Now present are âthe scribes,â who constitute a group that studies the law and assists in legal cases (see 23:10; the Note on 5:21).280 Possibly the members of the temple police return to their posts before this meeting begins. In 23:13 and 24:20, the chief priests remain as the most identifiable group and the general term ârulersâ subsumes the others.

their council: The scene now opens at âtheir council,â which evidently is held in a customary place away from the home of the high priest. The Greek term is synedrion.281 Obviously, events transpire in the home of the high priest that Luke does not repeat, most notably the appearance of the false witnesses before the assembled group (see Matt. 26:59â61; Mark 14:55â59). Does not this omission indicate that Luke, at this point, is following a source other than Mark? Probably so. Surely he knows about these other witnesses. But Lukeâs apparent oversight is evidently intended to mark Jesus as the only witness whom they should hear.

saying: This term in the Greek text belongs to the following verse. More than this, by repeating this term, Luke gathers all of Jesusâ accusers into the same box. There are no dissenting voices (see the Note on 22:70).282 In contrast, the other accounts hold that the high priest alone asks the questions (see Matt. 26:62â63; Mark 14:60â61).

22:67 Art thou the Christ?: This query forms the question of the ages, mirroring in importance the questions of guests at a dinner (see 7:49), the Apostles on a calmed sea (see 8:25), and Herod Antipas in his perplexity, âwho is this . . . ?â (9:9). A more complex matter has to do with the authoritiesâ understanding of who âthe Christâ is. Evidently, whether sincerely held or not, they somehow equate this figure with the âSon of Godâ (22:70), as do devils (see 4:41). Mention of these titles suits Lukeâs broad purpose to show, on the lips of Jesusâ opponents, that he is more than an itinerant prophet.283

he said: Jesus begins the first of two recorded responses, the one long and the other short. These statements link to his promise to the Twelve that, when brought to trial, they will have opportunity to bear witness of him (see 21:12â13).284

If I tell you: Jesus touches on an issue that stands at the heart of Johnâs Gospel, whether Jesus can bear witness of himself, a question that he answers affirmatively (see John 7:18; 8:12â18). One of Jesusâ aims in speaking as he does to the gathered council members, of course, is to uncover their unwillingness to believe (see the Note on 22:71).285

ye will not believe: This condemnation reaches back to another scene, one in which Jesus tells the parable of the rich man and Lazarus, concluding that, because the rich manâs brothers do not receive âMoses and the prophets,â they will not believe âthough one rose from the deadâ (16:31; see the Note thereon). Effectively Jesus, who will himself soon rise from the dead, paints the authorities with the same brush of unbelief.

22:68 if I also ask you, ye will not answer me: One of Jesusâ techniques for leading hearers to understand who he is comes in the form of questions (see 20:3â4, 41â44).286 But he clearly senses that his accusersâ anger will not allow him to aid their comprehension. In effect, they remain as if blindfolded (see the Note on 22:64).

nor let me go: Jesus shows that he knows the final outcome of his opponentsâ plans (see the Note on 5:22). His words disclose the fact that they do not intend to offer him a fair hearing. Now that he is in their grasp, they will hold onto him until they manufacture a case against him.

22:69 Hereafter: The Greek expression literally means âfrom now on.â This language appears at other key junctures in Jesusâ ministry and other related events, heralding a new step forward (see 1:48; 5:10; 12:52; 22:18; Acts 18:6).287

the Son of man sit: In a final effort to bring his accusers to understanding, Jesus draws their minds to one of the results of his coming resurrectionâhis exaltation, an event that they will not witness. Unlike accounts that lay emphasis on Jesusâ return (see 21:27; Matt. 26:64; Mark 14:62), Lukeâs quotation here rests directly on Jesusâ resurrection and its resplendent result, a result featured in scripture (see Ps. 110:1).

22:70 Then said they all: Luke underscores the united sentiment of Jesusâ accusers (see the Note on 22:66).

the Son of God: This title, though paired with âthe Christâ (22:67) and âthe Son of manâ (22:69), carries its own distinctive meaning. In this passage, Jesus does not deny his accusersâ attribution of the title to himself. But they and he understand it differently. For them, it becomes the trigger for hailing him before Pilate (see 22:71â23:1); for him, it remains the identifying marker of who he is (see 1:32, 35; 8:28).288

Ye say: Jesus will bring forward this type of response to Pilateâs question (see 23:3). In this latter instance, Pilate does not see anything incriminating in Jesusâ words (see 23:4) whereas Jesusâ accusers do. Even so, this type of reply seems to be the way that Jesus affirms his true identity during the trials, placing the words in someone elseâs mouth, almost as if he makes the other person a second witness in a legal case. Importantly for a legal proceeding, he does not deny what his accusers say.

I am: Here Jesus indirectly applies the divine name I AM to himself (see Ex. 3:14). Upon those in the room, all of whom know the Bible well, the effect of his statement is electric and moves them to drag him to Pilate (see the Notes on 3:22 and 9:35).

22:71 What need we any further witness?: In Lukeâs recounting, only Jesus stands as a witness while Matthew and Mark mention others (see Matt. 26:59â61; Mark 14:55â59). By itself, Jesusâ testimony is enough to bring the council members to a decision whether to accept or reject him.289 According to the other synoptic Gospels, the council allows false witnesses into its midst (see Matt. 26:59â62; Mark 14:55â60). This question that Luke reports implies the prior presence of other witnesses.

we ourselves have heard of his own mouth: Jesusâ accusers accept his words as representing who he is, that is, that he bears witness of himself (see the Notes on 4:22; 22:67). Luke adds no other comment, letting their words of rejection stand to condemn them.

Analysis

Above all else, the Savior bears witness of himself. In a final moving effort to reach out to his Jewish opponents and to turn them to himself, he stands among them in their well-trimmed and decorated chamber and invites them to see him as he is. Part of the challenge for them, of course, is his disheveled appearance, including skin, clothing, and hair stained by his blood. But his words spoken into a quiet, large room resonate with one person, Joseph of Arimathea, who seems already to be acquainted with Jesusâ message (see the Notes on 23:50â51). The scene reminds us of the words of the condemned prophet Abinadi that reach the heart of one man, Alma the priest (see Mosiah 17:1â4).

It is morning and it is spring. In the freshening light, colors in nearby gardens come alive. Flowers are in bloom; pomegranate and fig trees stand draped in new green leaves. In this season, the one who again and again restores and refreshes life, who again and again speaks of life continuing beyond mortality, who again and again shows the way to life with God, stands condemned to death. But matters will not remain this way. Even though Jesusâ future looks bleak and dreary, he is in charge. And soon the beauty and splendor of his resurrection will overshadow the beauties of the earth.

Jesus chooses this moment, when the earth is renewed and fresh, to reveal himself as he really is to those who exert the most influence in his society. Their genuine grasp of his meaning lies in their condemning words: âWhat need we any further witness? for we ourselves have heard of his own mouthâ (22:71). They ask, âArt thou the Christ?â He replies that, though unobserved by council members, from now on âthe Son of man [will] sit on the right hand of the power of Godâ (22:67, 69). They ask, âArt thou then the Son of God?â In a response that exudes humility and yet draws in the majestic divine name, he answers, âYe say that I amâ (22:70). The councilâs questions come to the heart of the matter; in turn, Jesusâ answers lead the council into a valley bathed by celestial sunlight. But they will not look. Instead of beholding the âlight . . . and the glory of [their] people Israelâ (2:32), they âwalk in the light of [their own meager] fire, and in the [faint] sparks that [they] have kindledâ (Isa. 50:11).

Perhaps oddly, Luke omits the scene that features false witnesses, whom the authorities assemble to accuse Jesus. Surely Luke knows about it, and surely he is aware that it begins as Peter and others bandy by the fire. But he turns away from it. Why? From what he preserves, it is apparent that he chooses to highlight Jesus as the main witness whom his accusers hear,290 as the single voice to whom they listen, as the royal person whom they reject (see the Notes on 22:66, 71; also Matt. 26:59â61; Mark 14:55â59). But they are rejecting more than a person who bears witness of himself. They are rejecting âthe Christ,â âthe Son of man,â âthe Son of God,â the great âI amâ (22:67, 69â70).

âFrom now on,â Jesus says of himself, âthe Son of man [will] sit on the right hand of the power of Godâ (see the Note on 22:69). But already in their turn, the authorities are the constant force in bringing Jesus to his execution by placing him in Romeâs power. In the council chamber, they make a decision that propels events into Pilateâs temporary residence in the city and eventually to Calvary. Unwittingly, they make a decision that brings about the will of God, even as they drag Jesus to Pilate and argue noisily for his death and join the soldiers that lead him to the place of execution (see 23:35).291 Never does one decision mean so much.

With the other Gospel writers, Luke shares only a few details in 22:63â64 and the material in 22:69. Everything else is unique to his report. This observation, resting on Lukeâs sweeping, transcendent view of what happens in the hearing, leads us to conclude that his account rests on other information. In no way does he appear to depend on either Matthew or Mark, even though points of similarity exist. For instance, in 22:63 Luke writes that âthe men that held Jesus mocked him, and smote him.â Like Luke, Matthew and Mark paint a scene of the arresting party physically roughing Jesus up. Like Luke, Mark, not Matthew, writes that someone covers Jesusâ face. Like Luke, both Matthew and Mark record that the crowd howls for a prophecy from Jesus, Matthew and Luke adding the detail that someone demands that Jesus identify the persons who strike him (see Matt. 26:67â68; Mark 14:65). But the differences are too compelling, including, for starters, the setting of the hearingââit was dayâ and the hearing occurs at âtheir councilâ room (22:66).292 A second example consists of the high priestâs dramatic tearing of his garments and his screeching accusation that Jesus utters blasphemy, pieces of the story that Luke does not include (see Matt. 26:65; Mark 14:63â64).293 The whole tenor of Lukeâs report is different. In his account we behold more clearly that Jesus is in charge. This clarity leads to one scholarâs conclusion that âthe sequence of events during that awful night [of arrest and trial] given in Luke makes sense, while in Mark (and Matthew) the description is at the very least strange and confused.â294
